Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array $a$ of length $n$, and an integer $x$. You can perform the following operation as many times as you would like (possibly zero): replace two adjacent elements of the array by their sum. For example, if the initial array was $[3, 6, 9]$, in a single operation one can replace the last two elements by their sum, yielding an array $[3, 15]$, or replace the first two elements to get an array $[9, 9]$. Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Please determine the minimum and the maximum beauty you can get by performing some operations on the original array.


-----Input-----

The first input line contains a single integer $t$ — the number of test cases ($1 \le t \le 1000$).

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$, $1 \leq x \leq 10^9$).

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each test case output two integers — the minimal and the maximal possible beauty.


-----Examples-----

Input
2
3 3
3 6 9
3 3
6 4 11
Output
6 6
7 8


-----Note-----

In the first test case the beauty of the array does not change if we perform any operations.

In the second example we can leave the array unchanged to attain the maximum beauty, and to get the minimum beauty one can replace two elements $4$ and $11$ with their sum, yielding an array $[6, 15]$, which has its beauty equal to $7$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. The beauty of an array is the sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
4. We need to find the minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations (keep the original array)
- This is because ceiling function is non-decreasing, and combining elements might reduce the total ceiling sum due to the ceiling property

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine adjacent elements strategically
- The key insight is that we can think about the total sum and how ceiling operations work
- For any set of positive numbers, the sum of ceiling of their values is always at least the ceiling of their sum
- However, we can achieve the minimum beauty by combining elements optimally

Let me think more deeply about the minimum beauty:

- If we combine two elements a and b, the beauty changes from ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ to ⌈(a+b)/x⌉
- We know that ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ (this is a mathematical property of ceiling functions)
- So combining elements never increases the beauty, but might decrease it
- The minimum beauty will be at least ⌈(sum of all elements)/x⌉ because we can combine everything into one element

But is ⌈(sum of all elements)/x⌉ always achievable? Yes, by combining all elements into a single element.

However, we need to check if we can get lower than this. Actually, no - the sum of ceilings is always at least the ceiling of the total sum, due to the property that ⌈a⌉ + ⌈b⌉ ≥ ⌈a+b⌉.

Therefore:
- Maximum beauty = sum of ⌈a_i/x⌉ for all i
- Minimum beauty = ⌈(sum of all a_i)/x⌉

Let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1:** [3,6,9], x=3
- Max beauty: ⌈3/3⌉ + ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈9/3⌉ = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Min beauty: ⌈(3+6+9)/3⌉ = ⌈18/3⌉ = 6
- Result: 6 6 ✓

**Example 2:** [6,4,11], x=3  
- Max beauty: ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈4/3⌉ + ⌈11/3⌉ = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Min beauty: ⌈(6+4+11)/3⌉ = ⌈21/3⌉ = 7
- Result: 7 8 ✓

Perfect! This matches the expected output.

## Solution

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
    max_beauty = 0
    for val in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(val / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceiling of total sum divided by x
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array length)
3. Beauty is defined as the sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations (keep original array)
- This is because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≤ ceil((a+b)/x) in many cases, but we need to check when it's possible to get higher values

Wait, let me think again. Actually:
- ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) (this is always true by properties of ceiling function)
- So breaking elements into smaller ones can actually reduce the total beauty

Actually, let me reconsider:
- ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This means merging two elements reduces or maintains beauty
- Therefore, the maximum beauty should be achieved when we don't merge anything (keep original array)

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to merge elements strategically
- We can merge adjacent elements arbitrarily
- The key insight is that we want to merge elements in a way that minimizes the sum of ceilings

However, there's a crucial observation: when we merge two elements, we get:
- Original: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)
- After merge: ceil((a+b)/x)

Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), merging always reduces or maintains the beauty.

Therefore:
- **Minimum beauty**: achieved when all elements are merged into a single element (only one element left: sum of array)
- **Maximum beauty**: achieved when no merges are performed (original array)

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Merged to [18]: ceil(18/3) = 6
- So both min and max are 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3  
- Original beauty: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Merged to [21]: ceil(21/3) = 7
- So min is 7, max is 8 ✓

This matches the expected output!

## Algorithm

1. **Maximum beauty**: sum of ceil(a_i / x) for all elements in original array
2. **Minimum beauty**: ceil(total_sum / x) where total_sum is sum of all array elements

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: no merges - original array
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: merge everything into one element
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

Wait, let me double-check if merging can ever give us a value lower than ceil(total_sum/x).

By the property of ceiling function:
ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

This is a standard inequality. By induction, since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), we have:
- The sum of ceilings of individual elements is always ≥ the ceiling of their sum

Therefore, merging elements never increases the beauty, and the minimum beauty is always achieved when all elements are merged into one.

So the solution is correct.

```python
import math

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: no merges - original array
    max_beauty = sum(math.ceil(num / x) for num in a)
    
    # Minimum beauty: merge everything into one element
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    print(min_beauty, max_beauty)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size)
3. Beauty = sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to minimize the "waste" of ceiling operations
- When we combine two elements, their ceiling sum is at most the sum of their individual ceilings
- However, we need to check if combining elements ever increases beauty
- Actually, combining elements can't increase beauty because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- So maximum beauty is achieved when we don't combine any elements at all
- Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine adjacent elements strategically
- When we combine two elements a and b, we get ceil((a+b)/x) which is ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)
- The key insight is that we can always achieve the minimum beauty by combining all elements into one
- The minimum beauty is ceil(total_sum/x)

Wait, let me verify this with the examples:

**Example 1**: [3,6,9], x=3
- Total sum = 18, ceil(18/3) = 6
- Individual ceilings: ceil(3/3)=1, ceil(6/3)=2, ceil(9/3)=3, sum=6
- So min = max = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: [6,4,11], x=3
- Total sum = 21, ceil(21/3) = 7
- Individual ceilings: ceil(6/3)=2, ceil(4/3)=2, ceil(11/3)=4, sum=8
- So min = 7, max = 8 ✓

This confirms my hypothesis!

## Algorithm

1. **Maximum beauty**: sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i
2. **Minimum beauty**: ceil(total_sum/x)

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i/x) for each element
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum/x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

The solution works because:

- **Maximum beauty**: We never want to combine elements since ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x), so the individual ceiling sum gives the maximum value.
- **Minimum beauty**: By combining all elements into one, we get the smallest possible value, which is ceil(total_sum/x). This is because combining elements reduces the number of terms in the sum, and ceiling operations are minimized when we have fewer larger terms.

The complexity is O(n) per test case, which is efficient given the constraints.

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. Beauty is defined as the sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find the minimum and maximum possible beauty after any number of operations

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to split elements as much as possible
- However, the ceiling function has a property: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This means combining elements can only decrease or keep the same beauty, never increase it
- Therefore, the maximum beauty is achieved when we don't combine any elements at all
- Maximum beauty = sum(ceil(a_i/x)) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements to reduce the total ceiling sum
- When we combine two elements a and b into (a+b), the beauty changes from ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) to ceil((a+b)/x)
- The key insight is that ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This means combining always reduces or maintains the beauty
- However, we can only combine adjacent elements, and we can combine multiple times

But wait - let me think more carefully about the minimum.

Actually, let's reconsider:
- The operation replaces two adjacent elements with their sum
- For any two positive numbers a, b: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- This means that combining elements never increases the total beauty
- So maximum beauty is achieved when no combinations occur (original array)

For minimum beauty, we need to find the most optimal way to combine elements.

The key insight is that the minimum beauty is not necessarily achieved by combining all elements into one. We need to check if there's a pattern.

Actually, let me think about this differently:
- When we combine elements, we're essentially creating larger elements
- Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), combining reduces beauty
- So we want to combine as much as possible to minimize beauty

But can we combine all elements into one? Yes, through a series of adjacent operations.

So the minimum beauty would be ceil(total_sum/x) where total_sum = sum(a_i).

Wait, is this always true? Let me verify:

For any partition of the array into groups, the beauty is sum(ceil(group_sum/x)).

And by the properties of ceiling functions, for any two groups with sums s1 and s2:
ceil(s1/x) + ceil(s2/x) ≥ ceil((s1+s2)/x)

So the total beauty is minimized when we combine all elements into a single group.

Therefore:
- Minimum beauty = ceil(sum(a_i)/x)
- Maximum beauty = sum(ceil(a_i/x))

Let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3**
- Sum = 18, ceil(18/3) = ceil(6) = 6
- Sum of ceil(ai/x) = ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Both are 6 ✓

**Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3**
- Sum = 21, ceil(21/3) = ceil(7) = 7
- Sum of ceil(ai/x) = ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Minimum = 7, maximum = 8 ✓

Perfect! This matches the expected output.

## Solution

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i / x)
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i / x)
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum / x)
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array $a$ of length $n$, and an integer $x$. You can perform the following operation as many times as you would like (possibly zero): replace two adjacent elements of the array by their sum. For example, if the initial array was $[3, 6, 9]$, in a single operation one can replace the last two elements by their sum, yielding an array $[3, 15]$, or replace the first two elements to get an array $[9, 9]$. Note that the size of the array decreases after each operation.

The beauty of an array $b=[b_1, \ldots, b_k]$ is defined as $\sum_{i=1}^k \left\lceil \frac{b_i}{x} \right\rceil$, which means that we divide each element by $x$, round it up to the nearest integer, and sum up the resulting values. For example, if $x = 3$, and the array is $[4, 11, 6]$, the beauty of the array is equal to $\left\lceil \frac{4}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{11}{3} \right\rceil + \left\lceil \frac{6}{3} \right\rceil = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8$.

Please determine the minimum and the maximum beauty you can get by performing some operations on the original array.


-----Input-----

The first input line contains a single integer $t$ — the number of test cases ($1 \le t \le 1000$).

The first line of each test case contains two integers $n$ and $x$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$, $1 \leq x \leq 10^9$).

The next line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \leq a_i \leq 10^9$), the elements of the array $a$.

It is guaranteed that the sum of values of $n$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^5$.


-----Output-----

For each test case output two integers — the minimal and the maximal possible beauty.


-----Examples-----

Input
2
3 3
3 6 9
3 3
6 4 11
Output
6 6
7 8


-----Note-----

In the first test case the beauty of the array does not change if we perform any operations.

In the second example we can leave the array unchanged to attain the maximum beauty, and to get the minimum beauty one can replace two elements $4$ and $11$ with their sum, yielding an array $[6, 15]$, which has its beauty equal to $7$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. Beauty of an array = sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want as many "ceil operations" as possible
- Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), the maximum beauty is achieved when we don't combine any elements
- This gives us the original array: sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements strategically
- When we combine two elements a and b into (a+b), we get: ceil((a+b)/x)
- We need to compare ceil((a+b)/x) vs ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)
- The key insight: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)
- But when does equality hold? When a % x and b % x don't cause a carry-over that creates an extra ceiling
- Actually, ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) always holds
- The minimum beauty is achieved when we combine elements optimally to minimize the sum
- Since ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x), we can keep combining until we get just one element
- The minimum beauty will be ceil(total_sum / x)

Wait, let me reconsider this. When we combine elements, we're replacing two elements with their sum. The beauty function has the property that:
- ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

This means that combining elements never decreases the beauty - it can only increase or stay the same. 

But actually, let me check: ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 4 = 6, while ceil((4+11)/3) = ceil(15/3) = 5. So 6 ≥ 5, meaning combining decreases beauty!

I made a mistake. Actually: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

So combining elements can decrease beauty. The minimum beauty should be ceil(total_sum / x), since we can always combine everything into one element.

For maximum beauty, we want to avoid combining elements as much as possible. The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't combine anything, which is sum of ceil(a[i]/x).

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1+2+3 = 6
- Combined: ceil((3+6+9)/3) = ceil(18/3) = 6
- So min and max both 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Combined: ceil(21/3) = 7
- Min should be 7, max should be 8 ✓

This matches the expected output!

## Algorithm

1. **Maximum beauty**: Sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all elements
2. **Minimum beauty**: ceil(total_sum / x)

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: don't combine any elements
    max_beauty = 0
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: combine all elements into one
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_beauty, max_beauty = solve()
    print(min_beauty, max_beauty)
```

Wait, let me double-check the logic with the example:

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original: ceil(6/3)=2, ceil(4/3)=2, ceil(11/3)=4 → sum = 8 ✓
- Combined: ceil(21/3)=7 ✓

The key insight is that:
- Maximum beauty occurs without any combinations (we can't do better than this)
- Minimum beauty occurs when we combine everything into one element (we can't do better than this)

This is because of the inequality: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

So the final solution is correct.

```python
import math

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: don't combine any elements
    max_beauty = sum(math.ceil(num / x) for num in a)
    
    # Minimum beauty: combine all elements into one
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    print(min_beauty, max_beauty)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. Beauty is defined as sum of ceil(element/x) for all elements
4. We need to find the minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations - we keep the original array
- This is because ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) in general
- Actually, let me verify: ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x) is always true
- So maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements optimally
- The key insight is that we can combine adjacent elements in any order
- Since we can perform any number of operations, we can combine all elements into one
- The minimum beauty would be ceil(total_sum/x)

Wait, let me think more carefully about the minimum beauty.

Actually, let me reconsider: can we always achieve ceil(sum/x) as the minimum beauty?

The beauty of the final array when all elements are combined is ceil(total_sum/x).

But is this always the minimum? Let's check with the examples.

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- If we combine all: ceil((3+6+9)/3) = ceil(18/3) = 6
- So minimum = 6, maximum = 6

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- If we combine all: ceil((6+4+11)/3) = ceil(21/3) = 7
- So minimum = 7, maximum = 8

This matches the expected output!

But is ceil(sum/x) always the minimum? 

The key insight is that when we combine elements, we might reduce the total beauty due to the ceiling function property:
ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x) ≥ ceil((a+b)/x)

This means that combining elements never increases the beauty, and can only decrease it or keep it the same.

Therefore, the minimum beauty is exactly ceil(sum of all elements / x).

However, we need to verify if this is indeed achievable. Since we can perform any sequence of operations on adjacent elements, we can eventually combine all elements into one. This means we can achieve beauty = ceil(total_sum/x).

## Solution

1. Maximum beauty = sum of ceil(a_i/x) for all i
2. Minimum beauty = ceil(total_sum/x)

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceil(a_i/x) for each element
    max_beauty = 0
    total_sum = 0
    
    for num in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(num / x)
        total_sum += num
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceil(total_sum/x)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length n and an integer x
2. We can replace any two adjacent elements with their sum (reducing array size by 1 each time)
3. Beauty = sum of ⌈element/x⌉ for all elements
4. We need to find minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we want to keep elements as separate as possible
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations (keep original array)
- This is because ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉ (by the property of ceiling function)
- So maximum beauty = Σ⌈a_i/x⌉ for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements optimally
- When we combine two elements a and b into (a+b), the beauty changes from ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ to ⌈(a+b)/x⌉
- Since ⌈a/x⌉ + ⌈b/x⌉ ≥ ⌈(a+b)/x⌉, combining reduces beauty
- The minimum beauty will be ⌈total_sum/x⌉ where total_sum is the sum of all elements

Why is this optimal?
- Any sequence of operations will end up with a single element equal to the total sum
- The beauty of a single element is ⌈total_sum/x⌉
- Any intermediate step before combining everything will have beauty ≥ ⌈total_sum/x⌉
- So ⌈total_sum/x⌉ is the minimum possible beauty

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For maximum beauty: sum of ⌈a_i/x⌉ for each element
2. For minimum beauty: ⌈(sum of all elements)/x⌉

## Edge Cases to Consider
- When x is very large, ⌈a_i/x⌉ = 1 if a_i > 0 (since a_i ≥ 1)
- When x is small, we need to compute ceiling properly

## Implementation

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: sum of ceiling of each element divided by x
    max_beauty = 0
    for element in a:
        max_beauty += math.ceil(element / x)
    
    # Minimum beauty: ceiling of total sum divided by x
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:** [3, 6, 9], x = 3
- Max beauty: ⌈3/3⌉ + ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈9/3⌉ = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Min beauty: ⌈(3+6+9)/3⌉ = ⌈18/3⌉ = 6
- Result: 6 6 ✓

**Example 2:** [6, 4, 11], x = 3  
- Max beauty: ⌈6/3⌉ + ⌈4/3⌉ + ⌈11/3⌉ = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Min beauty: ⌈(6+4+11)/3⌉ = ⌈21/3⌉ = 7
- Result: 7 8 ✓

The solution matches the expected output!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of length `n` and an integer `x`
2. We can replace two adjacent elements with their sum any number of times
3. The beauty of an array is the sum of ceil(element/x) for each element
4. We need to find the minimum and maximum possible beauty

## Key Insights

### Maximum Beauty
- To maximize beauty, we should keep elements as separate as possible
- The maximum beauty is achieved when we don't perform any operations (keep original array)
- This gives us the sum of ceil(a[i]/x) for all i

### Minimum Beauty
- To minimize beauty, we want to combine elements to reduce the sum of ceilings
- When we combine two elements, their sum's ceiling might be less than the sum of individual ceilings
- For any two elements a and b: ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)
- The equality holds when (a+b) is divisible by x or when the remainders when divided by x don't cause overflow
- To minimize beauty, we should combine elements strategically

## Pattern Analysis

The key insight is that we can always combine elements to minimize beauty. The minimum beauty depends on the total sum modulo x.

Let's think about the sum of all elements:
- Let S = sum of all elements
- The beauty of the final single element (after combining everything) is ceil(S/x)

But we can't always achieve this minimum because combining elements might not always reduce the beauty. However, we can prove that the minimum beauty is at least ceil(S/x).

Actually, let me reconsider: when we combine elements, the ceiling function has the property that:
- ceil((a+b)/x) ≤ ceil(a/x) + ceil(b/x)
- With equality when (a%x + b%x) < x, and strict inequality otherwise

The minimum beauty we can achieve is actually ceil(S/x), because:
- We can always combine all elements into one to get ceil(S/x)
- This is the smallest possible beauty since each element contributes at least ceil(a_i/x) to the sum

But wait - is ceil(S/x) always achievable? Yes, by combining all adjacent elements step by step.

And is it optimal? Yes, because any combination will have beauty at least ceil(S/x) due to the subadditive property of the ceiling function.

## Verification with Examples

Example 1: [3,6,9], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(3/3) + ceil(6/3) + ceil(9/3) = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6
- Total sum: 18, ceil(18/3) = 6
- Answer: 6 6 ✓

Example 2: [6,4,11], x=3
- Original beauty: ceil(6/3) + ceil(4/3) + ceil(11/3) = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8
- Total sum: 21, ceil(21/3) = 7
- Answer: 7 8 ✓

## Solution

```python
import math

def solve():
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty is when we don't combine anything
    max_beauty = sum(math.ceil(val / x) for val in a)
    
    # Minimum beauty is when we combine everything into one element
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    return min_beauty, max_beauty

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    min_b, max_b = solve()
    print(min_b, max_b)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the minimum beauty. Let me verify if ceil(total_sum/x) is always achievable and is indeed the minimum.

Yes, it is achievable by repeatedly combining adjacent elements until only one remains. And it's the minimum because:
- Each element contributes at least ceil(a_i/x) to the sum
- But when combined, the ceiling of a sum can be less than the sum of individual ceilings
- The total sum of ceilings is minimized when we have a single element with value total_sum

Therefore, the minimum beauty is exactly ceil(total_sum/x).

The maximum beauty is achieved when no operations are performed, which gives us sum(ceil(a_i/x)).

Final solution:

```python
import math

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x = map(int, input().split())
    a = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # Maximum beauty: when no operations are performed
    max_beauty = sum(math.ceil(val / x) for val in a)
    
    # Minimum beauty: when all elements are combined into one
    total_sum = sum(a)
    min_beauty = math.ceil(total_sum / x)
    
    print(min_beauty, max_beauty)
```
